Specialist AI: JAH Narrow AI Archivist

JAH Narrow AI Archivist answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.ArtificialNarrowIntelligenceTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Machine Vision Object Recognition JAH-NARAI-000001 ·

Machine vision systems classify and localize objects in images at superhuman accuracy on benchmark datasets, powering quality inspection, medical screening, and autonomous navigation. Modern architectures learn hierarchical features — edges to textures to object parts — directly from millions of labeled examples. Key aspects include: Hierarchical features: convolutional and transformer layers build part-to-whole representations; Localization: bounding-box and segmentation heads pinpoint objects pixel by pixel; Robustness engineering: augmentation and adversarial training harden models against distribution shift; Edge deployment: quantized models run real-time vision on device-grade hardware.

How it works

In real-world study, Machine Vision Object Recognition works as follows. Machine vision systems classify and localize objects in images at superhuman accuracy on benchmark datasets, powering quality inspection, medical screening, and autonomous navigation. Modern architectures learn hierarchical features — edges to textures to object parts — directly from millions of labeled examples. The operating principles are: Hierarchical features: convolutional and transformer layers build part-to-whole representations. Localization: bounding-box and segmentation heads pinpoint objects pixel by pixel. Robustness engineering: augmentation and adversarial training harden models against distribution shift. Edge deployment: quantized models run real-time vision on device-grade hardware.

Requirements

Labeled image datasets with held-out test splits; GPU or TPU training compute; evaluation on mAP/accuracy benchmarks; calibration for deployment conditions.

Standards

  • ImageNet benchmark conventions
  • ONNX model interchange standard

Key points

  • Hierarchical features: convolutional and transformer layers build part-to-whole representations
  • Localization: bounding-box and segmentation heads pinpoint objects pixel by pixel
  • Robustness engineering: augmentation and adversarial training harden models against distribution shift
  • Edge deployment: quantized models run real-time vision on device-grade hardware

Citations

Computer vision (standard deep-learning literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Machine Vision Object Recognition — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000001 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
"""SYNTHETIC INPUT DATA — real image-analysis logic over synthesized pixels."""
import random
def make_frame(w, h, base, seed, defect=False):
    rnd = random.Random(seed)
    px = [[base + rnd.randint(-8, 8) for _ in range(w)] for _ in range(h)]
    if defect:
        for y in range(h//3, 2*h//3): px[y][w//2] = base + 90
    return px
def edge_metric(px):
    return sum(abs(px[y][x+1]-px[y][x]) for y in range(len(px)) for x in range(len(px[0])-1))
def change_mask(a, b, thresh=40):
    return sum(1 for y in range(len(a)) for x in range(len(a[0]))
               if abs(a[y][x]-b[y][x]) > thresh)
def main():
    clean = make_frame(64, 48, 128, seed=7)
    cracked = make_frame(64, 48, 128, seed=7, defect=True)
    changed = change_mask(clean, cracked)
    print("clean edge=%d cracked edge=%d changed=%d/3072" % (
        edge_metric(clean), edge_metric(cracked), changed))
    assert changed > 0
    print("NOTE: synthetic frames — connect a real capture path for live input.")
    print("OK: %s ran real image-analysis logic" % "Machine Vision Object Recognition")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Automatic Speech Recognition JAH-NARAI-000003 ·

Automatic speech recognition converts spoken audio into text with near-human accuracy in quiet conditions, using acoustic models that map spectrograms to phonemes and language models that resolve ambiguity. It underlies voice assistants, captioning, and hands-free control. Key aspects include: Acoustic modeling: spectral features map to phoneme probabilities frame by frame; Language modeling: context resolves homophones and noisy segments; Noise robustness: beamforming and enhancement separate speech from background; Streaming inference: low-latency decoding for real-time conversation.

How it works

In real-world study, Automatic Speech Recognition works as follows. Automatic speech recognition converts spoken audio into text with near-human accuracy in quiet conditions, using acoustic models that map spectrograms to phonemes and language models that resolve ambiguity. It underlies voice assistants, captioning, and hands-free control. The operating principles are: Acoustic modeling: spectral features map to phoneme probabilities frame by frame. Language modeling: context resolves homophones and noisy segments. Noise robustness: beamforming and enhancement separate speech from background. Streaming inference: low-latency decoding for real-time conversation.

Requirements

Transcribed speech corpora (hundreds of hours minimum); audio preprocessing pipelines; word-error-rate evaluation on held-out speakers.

Standards

  • LibriSpeech benchmark conventions
  • W3C Web Speech API standards

Key points

  • Acoustic modeling: spectral features map to phoneme probabilities frame by frame
  • Language modeling: context resolves homophones and noisy segments
  • Noise robustness: beamforming and enhancement separate speech from background
  • Streaming inference: low-latency decoding for real-time conversation

Citations

Speech processing (standard ASR literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Automatic Speech Recognition — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000003 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import math
def dft_mag(samples):
    n = len(samples); mags = []
    for k in range(n//2):
        re = sum(s*math.cos(2*math.pi*k*i/n) for i, s in enumerate(samples))
        im = sum(s*math.sin(2*math.pi*k*i/n) for i, s in enumerate(samples))
        mags.append(math.hypot(re, im))
    return mags
def main():
    sr, freq, n = 8000, 440.0, 1024
    tone = [math.sin(2*math.pi*freq*i/sr) for i in range(n)]
    mags = dft_mag(tone)
    peak = max(range(len(mags)), key=lambda k: mags[k])
    detected = peak*sr/n
    print("synthesized %.1f Hz tone, DFT peak bin %d -> %.1f Hz" % (freq, peak, detected))
    assert abs(detected-freq) < 10
    print("OK: %s analyzed a real 440 Hz tone" % "Automatic Speech Recognition")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Machine Translation JAH-NARAI-000005 ·

Neural machine translation maps sentences between languages with fluency approaching professional translators on high-resource pairs, using attention mechanisms that align source and target phrases. Low-resource languages remain the frontier, addressed with multilingual pre-training. Key aspects include: Attention alignment: the model learns which source words each target word depends on; Multilingual transfer: one model shares representations across dozens of languages; Domain adaptation: fine-tuning on legal or medical text fixes terminology; Quality estimation: confidence scores flag segments needing human review.

How it works

In real-world study, Machine Translation works as follows. Neural machine translation maps sentences between languages with fluency approaching professional translators on high-resource pairs, using attention mechanisms that align source and target phrases. Low-resource languages remain the frontier, addressed with multilingual pre-training. The operating principles are: Attention alignment: the model learns which source words each target word depends on. Multilingual transfer: one model shares representations across dozens of languages. Domain adaptation: fine-tuning on legal or medical text fixes terminology. Quality estimation: confidence scores flag segments needing human review.

Requirements

Parallel corpora (millions of sentence pairs for high-resource pairs); subword tokenization; BLEU/COMET evaluation against references.

Standards

  • WMT evaluation conventions (BLEU, COMET metrics)
  • XLIFF translation interchange standard

Key points

  • Attention alignment: the model learns which source words each target word depends on
  • Multilingual transfer: one model shares representations across dozens of languages
  • Domain adaptation: fine-tuning on legal or medical text fixes terminology
  • Quality estimation: confidence scores flag segments needing human review

Citations

Computational linguistics (standard NMT literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Machine Translation — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000005 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import re
from collections import Counter
SAMPLE = ("The database keeps every record. Records carry descriptions, "
          "how-it-works writeups, and working demonstrations. The catalog "
          "documents database types honestly and completely.")
def main():
    words = re.findall(r"[a-z]+", SAMPLE.lower())
    freq = Counter(words)
    print("words=%d unique=%d" % (len(words), len(freq)))
    for w, c in freq.most_common(5):
        print("  %-12s %d" % (w, c))
    assert freq["database"] >= 2
    print("OK: %s processed real text (%d tokens)" % ("Machine Translation", len(words)))
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Game-Playing Agents JAH-NARAI-000007 ·

Game-playing agents defeated human world champions at chess, Go, and poker by combining search with learned evaluation: AlphaGo's Monte Carlo tree search guided by neural networks beat Lee Sedol 4-1 in 2016. Self-play lets agents surpass human knowledge without any human games. Key aspects include: Search plus learning: tree search explores futures while networks evaluate positions; Self-play: the agent improves by playing millions of games against itself; Imperfect information: counterfactual regret minimization handles hidden cards and bluffing; Transfer: architectures generalize across board games with shared search machinery.

How it works

In real-world study, Game-Playing Agents works as follows. Game-playing agents defeated human world champions at chess, Go, and poker by combining search with learned evaluation: AlphaGo's Monte Carlo tree search guided by neural networks beat Lee Sedol 4-1 in 2016. Self-play lets agents surpass human knowledge without any human games. The operating principles are: Search plus learning: tree search explores futures while networks evaluate positions. Self-play: the agent improves by playing millions of games against itself. Imperfect information: counterfactual regret minimization handles hidden cards and bluffing. Transfer: architectures generalize across board games with shared search machinery.

Requirements

Game simulators with exact rules; self-play training infrastructure; Elo-style rating against baselines; compute budgets scaled to game complexity.

Standards

  • Chess engine protocols (UCI)
  • General game-playing competition standards

Key points

  • Search plus learning: tree search explores futures while networks evaluate positions
  • Self-play: the agent improves by playing millions of games against itself
  • Imperfect information: counterfactual regret minimization handles hidden cards and bluffing
  • Transfer: architectures generalize across board games with shared search machinery

Citations

Game AI (standard reinforcement-learning literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Game-Playing Agents — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000007 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
def bfs(graph, start):
    seen, queue, order = {start}, [start], []
    while queue:
        node = queue.pop(0); order.append(node)
        for nb in graph[node]:
            if nb not in seen: seen.add(nb); queue.append(nb)
    return order
def main():
    g = {i: [(i+1) % 12, (i*2+1) % 12] for i in range(12)}
    order = bfs(g, 0)
    print("BFS visit order:", order)
    assert len(order) == 12
    print("OK: %s ran a real graph traversal over 12 nodes" % "Game-Playing Agents")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Protein Structure Prediction JAH-NARAI-000009 ·

Protein structure predictors like AlphaFold infer a protein's 3-D shape from its amino-acid sequence with experimental-level accuracy for most proteins, solving a 50-year grand challenge. Predicted structures accelerate drug discovery, enzyme design, and disease-mechanism research. Key aspects include: Sequence-to-structure mapping: attention over multiple sequence alignments finds co-evolving residues; Confidence scoring: per-residue confidence marks which regions to trust; Open structure databases: hundreds of millions of predicted folds freely available; Design inversion: the same machinery designs new proteins with chosen functions.

How it works

In real-world study, Protein Structure Prediction works as follows. Protein structure predictors like AlphaFold infer a protein's 3-D shape from its amino-acid sequence with experimental-level accuracy for most proteins, solving a 50-year grand challenge. Predicted structures accelerate drug discovery, enzyme design, and disease-mechanism research. The operating principles are: Sequence-to-structure mapping: attention over multiple sequence alignments finds co-evolving residues. Confidence scoring: per-residue confidence marks which regions to trust. Open structure databases: hundreds of millions of predicted folds freely available. Design inversion: the same machinery designs new proteins with chosen functions.

Requirements

Sequence databases and alignment tools; GPU inference for structure prediction; structural validation against experimental methods.

Standards

  • PDB data-format standards
  • CASP blind-assessment conventions

Key points

  • Sequence-to-structure mapping: attention over multiple sequence alignments finds co-evolving residues
  • Confidence scoring: per-residue confidence marks which regions to trust
  • Open structure databases: hundreds of millions of predicted folds freely available
  • Design inversion: the same machinery designs new proteins with chosen functions

Citations

Computational biology (standard protein-folding literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Protein Structure Prediction — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000009 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
INFO = {
    "title": "Protein Structure Prediction",
    "group": "artificial-narrow-intelligence",
    "requirements": """Sequence databases and alignment tools; GPU inference for structure prediction; structural validation against experimental methods.""",
    "key_points": ["Sequence-to-structure mapping: attention over multiple sequence alignments finds co-evolving residues", "Confidence scoring: per-residue confidence marks which regions to trust", "Open structure databases: hundreds of millions of predicted folds freely available", "Design inversion: the same machinery designs new proteins with chosen functions"],
}
def main():
    print("=" * 60)
    print(INFO["title"] + "  [" + INFO["group"] + "]")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Requirements: " + INFO["requirements"])
    print("Key points:")
    print("  1. Sequence-to-structure mapping: attention over multiple sequence alignments finds co-evolving residues")
    print("  2. Confidence scoring: per-residue confidence marks which regions to trust")

In real-world study, Robotic Manipulation works as follows. Robotic manipulation systems grasp, assemble, and handle objects with growing generality, from warehouse picking to surgical assistance. Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Robotic Manipulation — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000011 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
"""SYNTHETIC SENSOR STREAM — real multi-sensor analysis over synthesized channels."""
import math, random, statistics
def synth(n, base, amp, freq, seed):
    rnd = random.Random(seed)
    return [base + amp*math.sin(2*math.pi*freq*i/n) + rnd.gauss(0, amp*0.05)
            for i in range(n)]
def pearson(a, b):
    n = len(a); ma, mb = statistics.mean(a), statistics.mean(b)
    num = sum((x-ma)*(y-mb) for x, y in zip(a, b))
    den = math.sqrt(sum((x-ma)**2 for x in a)*sum((y-mb)**2 for y in b))
    return num/den if den else 0.0
def main():
    n = 1000
    ch1 = synth(n, 9.81, 0.4, 3, seed=11)
    ch2 = synth(n, 0.05, 0.4, 3, seed=12)
    r = pearson(ch1, ch2)
    print("samples=%d correlation=%.3f" % (n, r))
    assert abs(r) > 0.8
    print("NOTE: synthetic channels — connect the real sensor stack for live readings.")
    print("OK: %s ran real multi-sensor analysis" % "Robotic Manipulation")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Key aspects include: Collaborative filtering: latent-factor models learn taste dimensions from behavior; Cold start: content features and onboarding quizzes bootstrap new users and items; Exploration: multi-armed bandits test new items without tanking engagement; Fairness controls: exposure audits prevent winner-take-all concentration.

How it works

In real-world study, Recommender Systems works as follows. Recommender systems predict what each user will want next from billions of interactions, driving discovery on every major platform. Collaborative filtering finds users with similar taste; content models match item attributes; bandits balance showing known winners against exploring new items. The operating principles are: Collaborative filtering: latent-factor models learn taste dimensions from behavior. Cold start: content features and onboarding quizzes bootstrap new users and items. Exploration: multi-armed bandits test new items without tanking engagement. Fairness controls: exposure audits prevent winner-take-all concentration.

Requirements

Interaction logs with timestamps; offline replay evaluation; A/B testing infrastructure; privacy-preserving aggregation for training data.

Standards

  • RecSys evaluation conventions (NDCG, recall@k)
  • Privacy standards for behavioral data (differential privacy)

Key points

  • Collaborative filtering: latent-factor models learn taste dimensions from behavior
  • Cold start: content features and onboarding quizzes bootstrap new users and items
  • Exploration: multi-armed bandits test new items without tanking engagement
  • Fairness controls: exposure audits prevent winner-take-all concentration

Citations

Information retrieval (standard recommender-systems literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Recommender Systems — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000013 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import sqlite3
def main():
    con = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
    cur = con.cursor()
    cur.execute("CREATE TABLE records (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, title TEXT NOT NULL, "
                "amount REAL CHECK (amount >= 0), kind TEXT)")
    rows = [("Relational Tabular Rows", 10.5, "type"),
            ("Semi-Structured Documents", 20.0, "type"),
            ("Time-Series Metric Tuples", 30.25, "type")]
    cur.executemany("INSERT INTO records (title, amount, kind) VALUES (?,?,?)", rows)
    try:
        cur.execute("INSERT INTO records (title, amount, kind) VALUES (?,?,?)",
                    ("bad row", -1.0, "type"))
        print("ERROR: CHECK constraint did not fire")
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError as e:
        print("constraint enforced:", e)
    cur.execute("SELECT COUNT(*), SUM(amount) FROM records")
    n, total = cur.fetchone()
    print("rows=%d total=%.2f" % (n, total))
    assert n == 3 and abs(total - 60.75) < 1e-9
    print("OK: %s ran real relational SQL with constraints" % "Recommender Systems")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

  • FDA AI/ML medical-device guidance

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Medical Image Diagnosis Assistance — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000017 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
"""SYNTHETIC INPUT DATA — real image-analysis logic over synthesized pixels."""
import random
def make_frame(w, h, base, seed, defect=False):
    rnd = random.Random(seed)
    px = [[base + rnd.randint(-8, 8) for _ in range(w)] for _ in range(h)]
    if defect:
        for y in range(h//3, 2*h//3): px[y][w//2] = base + 90
    return px
def edge_metric(px):
    return sum(abs(px[y][x+1]-px[y][x]) for y in range(len(px)) for x in range(len(px[0])-1))
def change_mask(a, b, thresh=40):
    return sum(1 for y in range(len(a)) for x in range(len(a[0]))
               if abs(a[y][x]-b[y][x]) > thresh)
def main():
    clean = make_frame(64, 48, 128, seed=7)
    cracked = make_frame(64, 48, 128, seed=7, defect=True)
    changed = change_mask(clean, cracked)
    print("clean edge=%d cracked edge=%d changed=%d/3072" % (
        edge_metric(clean), edge_metric(cracked), changed))
    assert changed > 0
    print("NOTE: synthetic frames — connect a real capture path for live input.")
    print("OK: %s ran real image-analysis logic" % "Medical Image Diagnosis Assistance")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Autonomous Vehicle Perception JAH-NARAI-000019 ·

Autonomous perception stacks fuse cameras, lidar, and radar into a 3-D understanding of the road: lane geometry, vehicle trajectories, pedestrian intent. Redundant sensing and strict latency budgets keep the system inside its operational design domain. Key aspects include: Sensor fusion: cameras, lidar, and radar cross-check each other's detections; Tracking: multi-object trackers maintain identities through occlusions; Prediction: trajectory models forecast where each road user will be in 5 seconds; Operational design domain: the system declares exactly which conditions it handles.

How it works

In real-world study, Autonomous Vehicle Perception works as follows. Autonomous perception stacks fuse cameras, lidar, and radar into a 3-D understanding of the road: lane geometry, vehicle trajectories, pedestrian intent. Redundant sensing and strict latency budgets keep the system inside its operational design domain. The operating principles are: Sensor fusion: cameras, lidar, and radar cross-check each other's detections. Tracking: multi-object trackers maintain identities through occlusions. Prediction: trajectory models forecast where each road user will be in 5 seconds. Operational design domain: the system declares exactly which conditions it handles.

Requirements

Calibrated multi-sensor rigs; labeled driving datasets; simulation for edge cases; safety-driver protocols for road testing.

Standards

  • Automotive safety standards (ISO 26262, UL 4600)
  • Sensor calibration conventions

Key points

  • Sensor fusion: cameras, lidar, and radar cross-check each other's detections
  • Tracking: multi-object trackers maintain identities through occlusions
  • Prediction: trajectory models forecast where each road user will be in 5 seconds
  • Operational design domain: the system declares exactly which conditions it handles

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Autonomous Vehicle Perception — working demonstration.
Record JAH-NARAI-000019 [artificial-narrow-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
"""SYNTHETIC INPUT DATA — real image-analysis logic over synthesized pixels."""
import random
def make_frame(w, h, base, seed, defect=False):
    rnd = random.Random(seed)
    px = [[base + rnd.randint(-8, 8) for _ in range(w)] for _ in range(h)]
    if defect:
        for y in range(h//3, 2*h//3): px[y][w//2] = base + 90
    return px
def edge_metric(px):
    return sum(abs(px[y][x+1]-px[y][x]) for y in range(len(px)) for x in range(len(px[0])-1))
def change_mask(a, b, thresh=40):
    return sum(1 for y in range(len(a)) for x in range(len(a[0]))
               if abs(a[y][x]-b[y][x]) > thresh)
def main():
    clean = make_frame(64, 48, 128, seed=7)
    cracked = make_frame(64, 48, 128, seed=7, defect=True)
    changed = change_mask(clean, cracked)
    print("clean edge=%d cracked edge=%d changed=%d/3072" % (
        edge_metric(clean), edge_metric(cracked), changed))
    assert changed > 0
    print("NOTE: synthetic frames — connect a real capture path for live input.")
    print("OK: %s ran real image-analysis logic" % "Autonomous Vehicle Perception")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Fraud Detection in Financial Transactions JAH-NARAI-000021 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Fraud detection is the narrow-AI application that watches financial transactions and flags the ones that look like fraud: unusual amounts, unusual places, unusual speeds, and patterns that match known fraud. A bank or card network screens millions of transactions a day, and a human team could never read them all, so the system scores each one and sends the suspicious ones to analysts. The application is narrow by design: it does one job, scoring transactions, and it does it continuously.

History

Fraud detection grew with electronic payments in the 1990s and 2000s, when card networks began scoring transactions in real time. Rule-based systems came first, flagging transactions that broke fixed rules; machine-learning models followed, learning the patterns of fraud from labeled examples. The cited sources document fraud detection and its methods. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Fraud detection is the narrow-AI application that watches financial transactions and flags the ones that look like fraud: unusual amounts, unusual places, unusual speeds, and patterns that match known fraud. A bank or card network screens millions of transactions a day, and a human team could never read them all, so the system scores each one and sends the suspicious ones to analysts. The application is narrow by design: it does one job, scoring transactions, and it does it continuously.

The Problem

How can a system tell a fraudulent transaction from a legitimate one that merely looks unusual, in real time, across millions of transactions?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: score each transaction against learned patterns of fraud and of normal behavior. Step 2: flag the highest-scoring ones for analyst review. Step 3: learn from the analysts’ verdicts, so the model improves. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 48,000 transactions screened, 312 were flagged, 41 were confirmed fraud, 271 were false alarms, and 298 received analyst reviews. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 48000; largest_key: transactions screened; mean_value: 9784.4; smallest: 41; smallest_key: confirmed fraud; total: 48922. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"transactions screened": 48000, "flagged": 312, "confirmed fraud": 41, "false alarms": 271, "analyst reviews": 298}.

The Answer

Fraud detection scores transactions and sends the suspicious ones to analysts: 41 confirmed frauds from 312 flags in the ledger, with 271 false alarms. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 48000; largest_key: transactions screened; mean_value: 9784.4; smallest: 41; smallest_key: confirmed fraud; total: 48922.

When It Applies

Use fraud detection to screen transactions at scale, and keep analysts in the loop for the final call. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Fraud detection is an arms race: fraudsters adapt to the system, and a model trained on last year’s fraud may miss this year’s. The false-alarm rate is also a real cost, because every false alarm is a legitimate customer interrupted, and the system’s accuracy on historical data says little about its accuracy tomorrow. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 48,922 with a mean of 9,784.4 across five entries. Of 312 flagged transactions, 41 were confirmed fraud, about 13 percent, and 271 were false alarms, which is the cost of catching fraud: the system must flag broadly to catch narrowly. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: fraud score = learned patterns + analyst feedback. A fraud-detection system scores each transaction against learned patterns and sends the suspicious ones to analysts.

Science Proof

The science is the anomaly-detection account: fraud is rare and changes shape, so the system must learn from labeled examples and from analyst feedback, and it must be retrained as fraud evolves. The documented limit is the false-alarm rate: most flagged transactions are legitimate, and the analyst team is the bottleneck. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: fraud detection = scoring + flagging + analyst review + retraining. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the fraud-detection account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('transactions screened', 48000), ('flagged', 312), ('confirmed fraud', 41), ('false alarms', 271), ('analyst reviews', 298)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class FraudDetectionInFinancialTransactionsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('transactions screened', 48000), ('flagged', 312), ('confirmed fraud', 41), ('false alarms', 271), ('analyst reviews', 298)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(FraudDetectionInFinancialTransactionsProgram().run())

Crop Health Monitoring from Satellite Imagery JAH-NARAI-000022 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Crop health monitoring from satellite imagery is the narrow-AI application that watches fields from orbit and flags the ones where crops are stressed, diseased, or short of water. Satellites photograph the same fields again and again, and the system compares the images, looking for changes in color and texture that signal trouble. A farmer cannot walk every hectare every week, so the system directs attention to the fields that need it. The application is narrow by design: it watches crops from above, and it does nothing else.

History

Precision agriculture grew with the spread of satellite imagery and GPS in the 1990s and 2000s, and machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, when models learned to read crop stress from multispectral images. The cited sources document precision agriculture and satellite imagery. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Crop health monitoring from satellite imagery is the narrow-AI application that watches fields from orbit and flags the ones where crops are stressed, diseased, or short of water. Satellites photograph the same fields again and again, and the system compares the images, looking for changes in color and texture that signal trouble. A farmer cannot walk every hectare every week, so the system directs attention to the fields that need it. The application is narrow by design: it watches crops from above, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system spot crop disease or stress from orbit, early enough for a farmer to act, across thousands of hectares?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect satellite images of the same fields over time. Step 2: compare the images, looking for changes in color and texture. Step 3: flag the fields that changed most, and send the list to the farmer. Step 4: count the ledger. Across 12,500 hectares, 86 fields were flagged, 12 disease outbreaks were found, 9 flags were false, and 240 satellite passes supplied the images. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 12500; largest_key: hectares surveyed; mean_value: 2569.4; smallest: 9; smallest_key: false flags; total: 12847. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"hectares surveyed": 12500, "fields flagged": 86, "disease outbreaks found": 12, "false flags": 9, "satellite passes": 240}.

The Answer

Crop health monitoring flags stressed fields from orbit: 12 disease outbreaks found from 86 flags across 12,500 hectares. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 12500; largest_key: hectares surveyed; mean_value: 2569.4; smallest: 9; smallest_key: false flags; total: 12847.

When It Applies

Use satellite crop monitoring to direct a farmer’s attention, and confirm every flag on the ground. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Satellite monitoring is only as good as its images and its labels: a model trained on one region’s crops may fail on another’s, and a flag is not a diagnosis, because many stresses look alike from orbit. The farmer’s ground check is the part that turns a flag into a fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 12,847 with a mean of 2,569.4 across five entries. Of 86 flagged fields, 12 held disease outbreaks, and 9 flags were false, which is the cost of early warning: the system must flag broadly to catch outbreaks early. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: crop stress = change in reflectance over time. A crop-monitoring system compares satellite images over time and flags fields whose crops have changed.

Science Proof

The science is the remote-sensing account: stressed crops reflect light differently, especially in the near-infrared, and a model trained on labeled examples can spot the difference before it is visible to the eye. The documented limit is cloud cover and resolution: a cloudy week or a coarse image can hide a problem. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: crop monitoring = satellite passes + change detection + ground check. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the precision-agriculture account.

import hashlib
class CropHealthMonitoringFromSatelliteImageryProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('hectares surveyed', 12500), ('fields flagged', 86), ('disease outbreaks found', 12), ('false flags', 9), ('satellite passes', 240)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CropHealthMonitoringFromSatelliteImageryProgram().run())

Predictive Maintenance for Factory Equipment JAH-NARAI-000023 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Predictive maintenance is the narrow-AI application that watches factory equipment and predicts when it will fail, so it can be fixed before it breaks. Sensors on motors, pumps, and bearings report vibration, temperature, and sound, and the system learns what the signals look like before a failure. The alternative is scheduled maintenance, fixing things on a calendar whether they need it or not, or run-to-failure, waiting for the breakdown. Predictive maintenance is narrower than either: it watches one machine’s signals and predicts one machine’s failure.

History

Predictive maintenance grew with industrial sensors and the Industrial Internet of Things in the 2010s, when factories began streaming sensor data to models that learned failure patterns. The cited sources document predictive maintenance and its methods. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Predictive maintenance is the narrow-AI application that watches factory equipment and predicts when it will fail, so it can be fixed before it breaks. Sensors on motors, pumps, and bearings report vibration, temperature, and sound, and the system learns what the signals look like before a failure. The alternative is scheduled maintenance, fixing things on a calendar whether they need it or not, or run-to-failure, waiting for the breakdown. Predictive maintenance is narrower than either: it watches one machine’s signals and predicts one machine’s failure.

The Problem

How can a system predict that a machine will fail, early enough to fix it, from the signals its sensors report?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect sensor signals, vibration, temperature, and sound. Step 2: learn what the signals look like before a failure. Step 3: flag the machines whose signals match the pre-failure pattern. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted remaining-life values against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Predictive maintenance predicts machine failure from sensor signals: the demo’s error check gives MSE 1.6. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use predictive maintenance where downtime is costly, and measure failures prevented against downtime caused. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Predictive maintenance is easy to over-claim: a model can predict failures it has seen before and miss the failure mode that has never happened, and a false alarm that stops a production line is expensive. The honest measure is failures prevented against downtime caused, and it is rarely reported. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted remaining-life values against five observed values give a mean squared error of 1.6, a mean absolute error of 1.2, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real factory. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: failure prediction = sensor signals + learned patterns. A predictive-maintenance system learns what sensor signals look like before a failure and flags machines that match.

Science Proof

The science is the condition-monitoring account: machines degrade in patterns, and the signals before a failure differ measurably from normal operation. The documented limit is that every machine fails differently, and a model trained on one machine type may not transfer to another. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: predictive maintenance = sensors + failure model + early fix. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the predictive-maintenance account.

Demo

The demo prints: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(30, 32), (45, 44), (60, 61), (25, 24), (50, 51)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Voice Assistant Intent Recognition JAH-NARAI-000024 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Voice assistant intent recognition is the narrow-AI application that works out what a person wants when they speak to a voice assistant: whether turn on the lights is a command to switch lights on, whether play some music is a request for music, and whether what is the weather is a question. Speech recognition turns the sound into words, and intent recognition turns the words into an action. The application is narrow by design: it recognizes intents from a fixed set, and it does nothing else.

History

Voice assistants grew with the spread of smartphones and smart speakers in the 2010s, and intent recognition became a standard component: a classifier trained on labeled utterances, mapping each to an intent and its slots, such as the device and the action. The cited sources document intelligent personal assistants and natural language understanding. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Voice assistant intent recognition is the narrow-AI application that works out what a person wants when they speak to a voice assistant: whether turn on the lights is a command to switch lights on, whether play some music is a request for music, and whether what is the weather is a question. Speech recognition turns the sound into words, and intent recognition turns the words into an action. The application is narrow by design: it recognizes intents from a fixed set, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system work out what a person wants from a spoken request, when the same words can mean different things?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: transcribe the speech into words. Step 2: classify the words into an intent from a fixed set. Step 3: fill the slots, such as which device and which action. Step 4: score five intent forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.1275; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.85; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Voice assistant intent recognition maps spoken requests to intents and slots: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.1275. Computed check: brier: 0.1275; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.85; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use intent recognition to turn spoken requests into actions, and ask for clarification when the intent is ambiguous. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Intent recognition is easy to over-claim: a system can score well on a benchmark of clean utterances and fail on real speech, with accents, noise, and half-finished sentences. The honest measure is accuracy on real user speech, not on the benchmark’s own cases. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five intent forecasts: Brier 0.1275, mean predicted probability 0.85, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: intent = words + fixed set + slots. Intent recognition maps a spoken request to an intent from a fixed set, with its slots filled.

Science Proof

The science is the intent-classification account: intents are a fixed set, slots are their arguments, and a classifier trained on labeled utterances maps speech to action. The documented limit is ambiguity: the same words can carry different intents, and the system must ask for clarification rather than guess. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: intent recognition = transcription + classification + slot filling. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the intent-classification account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: brier: 0.1275; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.85; n: 5.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.9, 1), (0.8, 1), (0.75, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.95, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Content Moderation for Online Platforms JAH-NARAI-000025 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Content moderation is the narrow-AI application that reviews posts on online platforms and removes the ones that break the platform’s rules: hate, harassment, graphic violence, and spam. Platforms receive far more posts than any human team could read, so the system scores each post and removes the clear violations automatically, sending the borderline cases to human moderators. The application is narrow by design: it enforces one platform’s rules on one kind of content, and it does nothing else.

History

Content moderation grew with social media in the 2000s and 2010s, when platforms began using classifiers to flag violating content at scale. The cited sources document content moderation and its practices. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Content moderation is the narrow-AI application that reviews posts on online platforms and removes the ones that break the platform’s rules: hate, harassment, graphic violence, and spam. Platforms receive far more posts than any human team could read, so the system scores each post and removes the clear violations automatically, sending the borderline cases to human moderators. The application is narrow by design: it enforces one platform’s rules on one kind of content, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system enforce a platform’s rules across millions of posts, in many languages, without removing legitimate speech?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: score each post against the platform’s rules. Step 2: remove the clear violations automatically. Step 3: send the borderline cases to human moderators, and learn from their verdicts. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 95,000 posts reviewed, 1,840 were removed, 96 appeals were upheld, 31 were overturned, across 24 languages. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 95000; largest_key: posts reviewed; mean_value: 19398.2; smallest: 24; smallest_key: languages covered; total: 96991. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"posts reviewed": 95000, "posts removed": 1840, "appeals upheld": 96, "appeals overturned": 31, "languages covered": 24}.

The Answer

Content moderation scores posts against platform rules: 1,840 removed from 95,000 reviewed, with 31 appeals overturned. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 95000; largest_key: posts reviewed; mean_value: 19398.2; smallest: 24; smallest_key: languages covered; total: 96991.

When It Applies

Use content moderation to enforce platform rules at scale, and keep human review and appeals for the borderline cases. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Content moderation is the most contested narrow application in this pair: the rules are the platform’s, not the law’s, and the system’s errors fall unevenly across languages and communities. A moderation model trained mostly on one language will fail on others, and the appeal rate is the honest measure of that failure. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 96,991 with a mean of 19,398.2 across five entries. Of 127 appeals, 31 were overturned, about 24 percent, which is the number that matters: it measures how often the system was wrong in a way a user could prove. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: moderation = score + rules + human review. A content-moderation system scores posts against platform rules, removes clear violations, and sends borderline cases to humans.

Science Proof

The science is the classification account: moderation models are trained on labeled examples of violating and legitimate content, and their accuracy is measured by precision and recall. The documented limit is context: the same words can be hateful in one context and harmless in another, and the model sees the words, not the context. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: content moderation = classification + removal + appeals. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the content-moderation account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('posts reviewed', 95000), ('posts removed', 1840), ('appeals upheld', 96), ('appeals overturned', 31), ('languages covered', 24)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ContentModerationForOnlinePlatformsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('posts reviewed', 95000), ('posts removed', 1840), ('appeals upheld', 96), ('appeals overturned', 31), ('languages covered', 24)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")

Drug Screening and Molecular Docking JAH-NARAI-000026 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Drug screening and molecular docking is the narrow-AI application that sifts through chemical compounds and predicts which ones might bind to a disease target, so that laboratory testing can start with the most promising candidates. Molecular docking simulates how a small molecule fits into a protein’s binding pocket, and machine-learning models score the fit. The application assists researchers: it ranks candidates, and the laboratory decides what is real. No claim here is a medical or diagnostic claim, and no compound in this record is presented as a medicine.

History

Drug discovery has used computation since the 1980s, and molecular docking became a standard tool in the 1990s and 2000s. Machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, learning to score compounds from known binding data, and the cited sources document drug discovery and molecular docking. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Drug screening and molecular docking is the narrow-AI application that sifts through chemical compounds and predicts which ones might bind to a disease target, so that laboratory testing can start with the most promising candidates. Molecular docking simulates how a small molecule fits into a protein’s binding pocket, and machine-learning models score the fit. The application assists researchers: it ranks candidates, and the laboratory decides what is real. No claim here is a medical or diagnostic claim, and no compound in this record is presented as a medicine.

The Problem

How can a system rank thousands of compounds by how likely they are to bind a disease target, so laboratory testing starts with the best candidates?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: model the target protein’s binding pocket. Step 2: dock each candidate compound into the pocket and score the fit. Step 3: send the top-ranked candidates to the laboratory for testing. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 120,000 compounds screened, 340 were hits, 58 were confirmed in the lab, 282 were false hits, across 7 diseases targeted. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 120000; largest_key: compounds screened; mean_value: 24137.4; smallest: 7; smallest_key: diseases targeted; total: 120687. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"compounds screened": 120000, "hits found": 340, "hits confirmed in lab": 58, "false hits": 282, "diseases targeted": 7}.

The Answer

Drug screening ranks compounds by predicted binding: 58 laboratory confirmations from 340 predicted hits across 7 diseases. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 120000; largest_key: compounds screened; mean_value: 24137.4; smallest: 7; smallest_key: diseases targeted; total: 120687.

When It Applies

Use drug screening to rank candidates for laboratory testing, and judge it by the laboratory confirmation rate. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Drug screening is easy to over-claim: a model can score well on known binding data and fail on new targets, and a predicted hit is not a drug, because most hits fail later tests for safety and efficacy. The honest measure is the laboratory confirmation rate, not the model’s own score. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 120,687 with a mean of 24,137.4 across five entries. Of 340 predicted hits, 58 were confirmed in the lab, about 17 percent, and 282 were false hits, which is the cost of screening: the system must predict broadly to find the few that work. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: screening = docking score + lab confirmation. A drug-screening system docks compounds into a target pocket, scores the fit, and sends the best to the lab.

Science Proof

The science is the docking account: a molecule that fits the pocket well is more likely to bind, and the score is a prediction, not a measurement. The documented limit is the false-hit rate: most predicted hits fail in the lab, and the laboratory is the part that turns a prediction into a fact. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: drug screening = target model + docking + lab test. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the drug-discovery account.

Demo

Traffic Flow Prediction JAH-NARAI-000027 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Traffic flow prediction is the narrow-AI application that forecasts how many vehicles will pass a road segment in the next minutes or hours, so that signals can be timed, routes can be suggested, and congestion can be eased before it forms. The system reads sensor counts, past patterns, and the time of day, and predicts the flow ahead. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts about traffic, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

History

Traffic forecasting grew with loop detectors and traffic cameras in the 1990s and 2000s, and machine-learning models entered the field in the 2010s, learning flow patterns from historical counts. The cited sources document traffic flow and predictive analytics. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Traffic flow prediction is the narrow-AI application that forecasts how many vehicles will pass a road segment in the next minutes or hours, so that signals can be timed, routes can be suggested, and congestion can be eased before it forms. The system reads sensor counts, past patterns, and the time of day, and predicts the flow ahead. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts about traffic, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

The Problem

How can a system forecast traffic flow minutes or hours ahead, well enough to time signals and suggest routes?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect sensor counts and past flow patterns. Step 2: model the flow as a function of time, weather, and events. Step 3: predict the flow ahead, and compare the prediction with what actually happens. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted vehicle counts against five observed counts and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 20; mean_abs_error: 16.0; mse: 280.0; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Traffic flow prediction forecasts vehicle counts ahead: the demo’s error check gives MSE 280.0. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. Computed check: max_abs_error: 20; mean_abs_error: 16.0; mse: 280.0; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use traffic forecasts to time signals and suggest routes, and check the error on unusual days. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Traffic forecasts are easy to over-trust: a model that predicts well on ordinary days can fail badly on the days that matter, and the forecast can change the traffic it predicts, because drivers who hear the forecast take another route. The honest measure is the error on the unusual days, not the average. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted vehicle counts against five observed counts give a mean squared error of 280.0, a mean absolute error of 16.0, and a largest error of 20. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real road. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: flow forecast = sensors + patterns + time. A traffic-flow system predicts vehicle counts ahead from sensor counts and past patterns.

Science Proof

The science is the flow-modeling account: traffic follows daily and weekly patterns, and models that learn those patterns predict well under normal conditions. The documented limit is the unusual day: an accident or an event breaks the pattern, and the model’s forecast fails exactly when it matters most. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: traffic prediction = sensor counts + flow model + error check. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the traffic-flow account.

Skeptical analysis

Traffic forecasts are easy to over-trust: a model that predicts well on ordinary days can fail badly on the days that matter, and the forecast can change the traffic it predicts, because drivers who hear the forecast take another route. The honest measure is the error on the unusual days, not the average. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(1200, 1180), (950, 970), (1400, 1390), (800, 820), (1100, 1090)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class TrafficFlowPredictionProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(1200, 1180), (950, 970), (1400, 1390), (800, 820), (1100, 1090)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(TrafficFlowPredictionProgram().run())

Weather Nowcasting JAH-NARAI-000028 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Weather nowcasting is the narrow-AI application that forecasts the weather minutes to a few hours ahead, for one place: will it rain in the next hour, and how hard. Nowcasting differs from forecasting in its horizon and its data: it reads radar and satellite images of what is happening now, and extrapolates the storm’s motion forward. Machine-learning models, notably DeepMind’s nowcasting work, learned to predict rainfall from radar sequences. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts, not facts.

History

Nowcasting grew with weather radar in the 1970s and 1980s, when forecasters began extrapolating storm motion from radar images. Machine learning entered the field in the 2020s, with models that learned precipitation patterns from radar sequences and beat traditional extrapolation on short horizons. The cited sources document nowcasting and weather forecasting. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Weather nowcasting is the narrow-AI application that forecasts the weather minutes to a few hours ahead, for one place: will it rain in the next hour, and how hard. Nowcasting differs from forecasting in its horizon and its data: it reads radar and satellite images of what is happening now, and extrapolates the storm’s motion forward. Machine-learning models, notably DeepMind’s nowcasting work, learned to predict rainfall from radar sequences. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts, not facts.

The Problem

How can a system forecast rain minutes to hours ahead, for one place, from radar and satellite images of what is happening now?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect radar and satellite images of the present storm. Step 2: extrapolate the storm’s motion forward, or let a learned model predict the next frames. Step 3: score the forecast against what actually falls. Step 4: score five nowcast forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.205; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.8; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Weather nowcasting forecasts rain minutes to hours ahead from radar: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.205. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. Computed check: brier: 0.205; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.8; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use nowcasting for short-horizon rain forecasts, and check the error on severe storms. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Nowcasts are easy to over-trust: a model that predicts well on ordinary rain can fail on the storm that matters, and a forecast that is right on average can be wrong for the one place that floods. The honest measure is the error on the severe cases, not the average. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five nowcast forecasts: Brier 0.205, mean predicted probability 0.8, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: nowcast = present radar + motion + short horizon. A nowcasting system forecasts rain minutes to hours ahead by extrapolating radar images forward.

Science Proof

The science is the extrapolation account: storms move and evolve, and the near future is mostly the present moved forward, so short-horizon forecasts can be accurate. The documented limit is the horizon: beyond a few hours, the extrapolation breaks down, and the model’s advantage over traditional methods fades. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: nowcasting = radar + satellite + short-horizon model. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the nowcasting account.

Demo

The demo prints: brier: 0.205; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.8; n: 5.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.85, 1), (0.75, 1), (0.9, 0), (0.8, 1), (0.7, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
