Wildlife Population Monitoring JAH-NARAI-000029 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Wildlife population monitoring is the narrow-AI application that watches animals in the wild through camera traps, and counts them: which species, how many, where, and when. Camera traps photograph whatever walks past, and the system identifies the animals in the images, turning thousands of photographs into population counts. Conservationists cannot watch every trail, so the system does the counting, and rangers act on the alerts. The application is narrow by design: it identifies animals in camera-trap images, and it does nothing else.

History

Camera traps entered wildlife research in the 1990s, and machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, when models learned to identify species in trap images. The cited sources document camera traps and wildlife conservation. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Wildlife population monitoring is the narrow-AI application that watches animals in the wild through camera traps, and counts them: which species, how many, where, and when. Camera traps photograph whatever walks past, and the system identifies the animals in the images, turning thousands of photographs into population counts. Conservationists cannot watch every trail, so the system does the counting, and rangers act on the alerts. The application is narrow by design: it identifies animals in camera-trap images, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system count wild animals across a reserve, from thousands of camera-trap photographs, well enough to track populations?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: place camera traps along trails and water sources. Step 2: identify the animals in each image, with species and count. Step 3: aggregate the identifications into population counts, and alert rangers to poaching signs. Step 4: count the ledger. From 320 camera traps, 48,000 images were captured, 1,240 animals were identified, 42 species were recorded, and 6 poaching alerts were raised. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 48000; largest_key: images captured; mean_value: 9921.6; smallest: 6; smallest_key: poaching alerts; total: 49608. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"camera traps": 320, "images captured": 48000, "animals identified": 1240, "species recorded": 42, "poaching alerts": 6}.

The Answer

Wildlife monitoring counts animals from camera-trap images: 1,240 animals identified across 42 species from 48,000 images. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 48000; largest_key: images captured; mean_value: 9921.6; smallest: 6; smallest_key: poaching alerts; total: 49608.

When It Applies

Use wildlife monitoring to track populations across a reserve, and check counts against ground surveys. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Wildlife monitoring is only as good as its images and its labels: a model trained on one reserve’s animals may fail on another’s, and a count is not a population, because the same animal can be photographed many times. The honest measure is the count against a ground survey, not the model’s own confidence. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 49,608 with a mean of 9,921.6 across five entries. Of 48,000 images, 1,240 animals were identified, and 42 species were recorded, which is the population picture the system exists to produce. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: population count = trap images + identification + aggregation. A wildlife-monitoring system identifies animals in camera-trap images and aggregates them into population counts.

Science Proof

The science is the identification account: a model trained on labeled trap images can identify species as well as a human expert on clear images, and far faster. The documented limit is the unclear image: night shots, partial animals, and rare species are where the model fails, and those are the cases that matter most. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: wildlife monitoring = camera traps + species identification + counts. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the camera-trap account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: count: 5; largest: 48000; largest_key: images captured; mean_value: 9921.6; smallest: 6; smallest_key: poaching alerts; total: 49608.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('camera traps', 320), ('images captured', 48000), ('animals identified', 1240), ('species recorded', 42), ('poaching alerts', 6)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(WildlifePopulationMonitoringProgram().run())

Handwriting Recognition JAH-NARAI-000030 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Handwriting recognition is the narrow-AI application that reads handwritten text and turns it into typed text: letters, notes, forms, and historical documents. A human’s handwriting varies with the writer, the pen, and the mood, so the system must learn the shapes of letters as they are actually written, not as they are printed. The application is narrow by design: it reads handwriting, and it does nothing else.

History

Handwriting recognition grew with the first pen computers of the 1980s and 1990s, and deep learning transformed the field in the 2010s, when sequence models learned to read cursive handwriting from the IAM dataset and others. The cited sources document handwriting recognition. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Handwriting recognition is the narrow-AI application that reads handwritten text and turns it into typed text: letters, notes, forms, and historical documents. A human’s handwriting varies with the writer, the pen, and the mood, so the system must learn the shapes of letters as they are actually written, not as they are printed. The application is narrow by design: it reads handwriting, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system read handwriting, when every writer’s letters look different, and turn it into typed text?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled examples of handwritten text. Step 2: train a sequence model to map the image to characters. Step 3: check the output against the document, and flag the uncertain words. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted recognition scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.0; mse: 1.4; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Handwriting recognition reads handwritten text into typed text: the demo’s error check gives MSE 1.4. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.0; mse: 1.4; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use handwriting recognition to digitize handwritten documents, and flag uncertain words for human review. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Handwriting recognition is easy to over-claim: a model can score well on a clean dataset and fail on real notes, with crossed-out words, marginalia, and faded ink. The honest measure is accuracy on the documents that actually need reading, not on the benchmark’s own cases. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted recognition scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 1.4, a mean absolute error of 1.0, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real system. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: handwriting = image + sequence model + typed text. A handwriting-recognition system maps images of handwritten text to typed characters as a sequence.

Science Proof

The science is the sequence-model account: handwriting is a sequence, and models that read it as a sequence, rather than as isolated letters, do far better. The documented limit is the unusual writer: a model trained on many writers still fails on the writer it has never seen. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: handwriting recognition = labeled examples + sequence model + human check. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the handwriting-recognition account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(92, 91), (88, 89), (95, 94), (84, 86), (90, 90)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()

            print(f"{k}: {v}")

Sentiment Analysis of Customer Reviews JAH-NARAI-000031 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Sentiment analysis of customer reviews is the narrow-AI application that reads what customers write about a product and scores whether each review is positive, negative, or mixed. A business cannot read every review, so the system aggregates the sentiment: how customers feel overall, which features they praise, and which they complain about. The application is narrow by design: it scores sentiment in reviews, and it does nothing else. It measures what people write, not what they feel inside; the score is about the text, never a claim about a person’s inner state.

History

Sentiment analysis grew with online reviews in the 2000s, when businesses began mining review text for opinion. Machine-learning classifiers, and later language models, learned to score sentiment from labeled reviews. The cited sources document sentiment analysis. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Sentiment analysis of customer reviews is the narrow-AI application that reads what customers write about a product and scores whether each review is positive, negative, or mixed. A business cannot read every review, so the system aggregates the sentiment: how customers feel overall, which features they praise, and which they complain about. The application is narrow by design: it scores sentiment in reviews, and it does nothing else. It measures what people write, not what they feel inside; the score is about the text, never a claim about a person’s inner state.

The Problem

How can a system score the sentiment of a customer review, when the same words can praise one feature and complain about another?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled reviews, scored by human readers. Step 2: train a classifier to score new reviews. Step 3: aggregate the scores by feature, so the business sees what is praised and what is complained about. Step 4: score five sentiment forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.115; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.74; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Sentiment analysis scores reviews as positive, negative, or mixed: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.115. Computed check: brier: 0.115; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.74; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use sentiment analysis to aggregate review opinion by feature, and report mixed reviews separately. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Sentiment analysis is easy to over-trust: a model can score well on a benchmark of clear reviews and fail on sarcasm, irony, and mixed feelings, and a single score hides the mix that matters. The honest measure is accuracy on real reviews, with the mixed cases reported separately. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five sentiment forecasts: Brier 0.115, mean predicted probability 0.74, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: sentiment score = review text + classifier + aggregation. A sentiment-analysis system scores customer reviews and aggregates the scores by feature.

Science Proof

The science is the classification account: sentiment is scored from text, and the model’s accuracy is measured against human labels. The documented limit is sarcasm and mixed reviews: a review that praises one feature and damns another defeats a single score, and the model must report the mix, not force a verdict. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: sentiment analysis = labeled reviews + classifier + feature aggregation. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the sentiment-analysis account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.8, 1), (0.7, 1), (0.6, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.75, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Optical Character Recognition for Documents JAH-NARAI-000032 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Optical character recognition, or OCR, is the narrow-AI application that reads printed text in scanned documents and turns it into searchable typed text: books, forms, invoices, and archives. A scanned page is a picture, and OCR turns the picture into characters a computer can search, copy, and index. The application is narrow by design: it reads printed text, and it does nothing else.

History

OCR grew with scanners in the 1970s and 1980s, when the first systems read printed characters from documents, and deep learning transformed the field in the 2010s, when models learned to read varied fonts and layouts. The cited sources document optical character recognition. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Optical character recognition, or OCR, is the narrow-AI application that reads printed text in scanned documents and turns it into searchable typed text: books, forms, invoices, and archives. A scanned page is a picture, and OCR turns the picture into characters a computer can search, copy, and index. The application is narrow by design: it reads printed text, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system turn a scanned page, a picture of text, into searchable typed text, across fonts, layouts, and languages?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: scan the page into an image. Step 2: find the text regions and read the characters. Step 3: check the output against a dictionary, and flag the uncertain words. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 15,000 pages digitized, 4,200,000 characters were recognized with 8,400 errors, across 18 languages, completing 3,200 documents. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 4200000; largest_key: characters recognized; mean_value: 845323.6; smallest: 18; smallest_key: languages handled; total: 4226618. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The Answer

When It Applies

Limits

The honest measure is the error rate on the real archive, not on the benchmark’s own pages. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 4,226,618 with a mean of 845,323.6 across five entries. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: OCR = scanned image + text regions + characters. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('pages digitized', 15000), ('characters recognized', 4200000), ('recognition errors', 8400), ('languages handled', 18), ('documents completed', 3200)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class OpticalCharacterRecognitionForDocumentsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('pages digitized', 15000), ('characters recognized', 4200000), ('recognition errors', 8400), ('languages handled', 18), ('documents completed', 3200)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()

        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")

Chatbot Customer Support JAH-NARAI-000033 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Chatbot customer support is the narrow-AI application that answers customer questions in chat: order status, returns, passwords, and the common problems that have standard answers. The chatbot reads the customer’s message, matches it to a known issue, and replies with the answer or the steps to fix it, escalating to a human when the problem is new or the customer is unhappy. The application is narrow by design: it answers support questions from a fixed set, and it does nothing else.

History

Support chatbots grew with messaging apps and websites in the 2010s, when businesses began answering common questions automatically. Rule-based bots came first, matching keywords to answers; machine-learning models followed, classifying intents and generating replies. The cited sources document chatbots. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Chatbot customer support is the narrow-AI application that answers customer questions in chat: order status, returns, passwords, and the common problems that have standard answers. The chatbot reads the customer’s message, matches it to a known issue, and replies with the answer or the steps to fix it, escalating to a human when the problem is new or the customer is unhappy. The application is narrow by design: it answers support questions from a fixed set, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system answer customer support questions in chat, well enough to resolve the common ones and escalate the rest to a human?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: classify the customer’s message into a known issue. Step 2: reply with the answer or the fix steps. Step 3: escalate when the issue is new, the customer is unhappy, or the confidence is low. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 22,000 conversations handled, 15,400 were resolved without a human, 6,600 were escalated, with a satisfaction score of 82, across 12 languages. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 22000; largest_key: conversations handled; mean_value: 8818.8; smallest: 12; smallest_key: languages supported; total: 44094. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"conversations handled": 22000, "resolved without human": 15400, "escalated": 6600, "satisfaction score": 82, "languages supported": 12}.

The Answer

Chatbot customer support answers common questions in chat: 15,400 of 22,000 conversations resolved without a human. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 22000; largest_key: conversations handled; mean_value: 8818.8; smallest: 12; smallest_key: languages supported; total: 44094.

When It Applies

Use support chatbots for common questions, and keep a fast escalation path to a human. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Support chatbots are easy to over-claim: a bot can score well on common questions and fail on the ones that matter, and a customer trapped in a loop with a bot is worse off than a customer who waited for a human. The honest measure is resolution plus satisfaction, not the number of conversations handled. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 44,094 with a mean of 8,818.8 across five entries. Of 22,000 conversations, 15,400 were resolved without a human, 70 percent, and 6,600 were escalated, which is the safety valve: the system must know when to hand over. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: support bot = intent + answer + escalation. A support chatbot classifies customer messages into known issues, answers the common ones, and escalates the rest.

Science Proof

The science is the intent-classification account: support questions fall into a fixed set of issues, and a classifier trained on labeled conversations maps messages to answers. The documented limit is the new problem: a chatbot can only answer what it has been taught, and the escalation path is the part that makes it safe. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: chatbot support = intent classification + answers + human escalation. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the chatbot account.

Credit Scoring Models JAH-NARAI-000034 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Credit scoring models are the narrow-AI application that estimates how likely a borrower is to repay a loan, from their credit history: past borrowing, repayment, and defaults. A lender cannot read every application by hand, so the model scores each one, and the score guides the decision. The application is narrow by design: it scores credit risk, and it does nothing else. Its scores shape who gets credit, so fairness and accuracy are both part of the record, and the model’s errors fall on real people.

History

Credit scoring grew with the Fair Isaac score in the 1950s and 1960s, when statistical models first scored borrowers, and machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, learning scores from larger histories. The cited sources document credit scores and their history. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Credit scoring models are the narrow-AI application that estimates how likely a borrower is to repay a loan, from their credit history: past borrowing, repayment, and defaults. A lender cannot read every application by hand, so the model scores each one, and the score guides the decision. The application is narrow by design: it scores credit risk, and it does nothing else. Its scores shape who gets credit, so fairness and accuracy are both part of the record, and the model’s errors fall on real people.

The Problem

How can a system estimate the likelihood that a borrower repays, from their credit history, fairly and accurately?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: gather the credit history, past borrowing, repayment, and defaults. Step 2: train a model to score new applications. Step 3: check the score against outcomes, and audit it for bias across groups. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted credit scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 5; mean_abs_error: 3.2; mse: 12.4; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Credit scoring models estimate repayment likelihood from credit history: the demo’s error check gives MSE 12.4. Computed check: max_abs_error: 5; mean_abs_error: 3.2; mse: 12.4; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use credit scoring to guide lending decisions, with a fairness audit alongside the accuracy check. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Credit scoring is the most consequential narrow application in this pair: a wrong score denies a person credit, and a biased score denies it unevenly. A model can be accurate on average and unfair in its errors, and the honest measure is accuracy plus a fairness audit, never accuracy alone. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted credit scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 12.4, a mean absolute error of 3.2, and a largest error of 5. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real lender. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: credit score = history + model + audit. A credit-scoring model estimates repayment likelihood from credit history, audited for bias.

Science Proof

The science is the scoring account: past behavior predicts future repayment imperfectly, and the model’s accuracy is measured by how well its scores rank borrowers. The documented limit is fairness: a model trained on historical data inherits historical bias, and the audit is the part that catches it. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: credit scoring = history + scoring model + fairness audit. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the credit-score account.

Demo

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(720, 718), (650, 655), (800, 798), (580, 585), (690, 688)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()

import hashlib
class CreditScoringModelsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(720, 718), (650, 655), (800, 798), (580, 585), (690, 688)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Seismic Event Detection JAH-NARAI-000035 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Seismic event detection is the narrow-AI application that watches seismometer signals and picks out earthquakes, distinguishing them from noise, quarry blasts, and passing trucks. Seismometers record ground motion continuously, and the system must decide, in seconds, whether a wiggle is an earthquake worth reporting. The application is narrow by design: it detects seismic events, and it does nothing else. It reports what the ground did; it does not predict earthquakes, which remains beyond what the science can do.

History

Seismic detection grew with seismometer networks in the twentieth century, when analysts picked arrivals by hand. Machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, when models learned to pick earthquake signals from continuous waveforms, and earthquake early-warning systems began using those picks to warn seconds before the shaking arrives. The cited sources document seismology and earthquake early warning. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Seismic event detection is the narrow-AI application that watches seismometer signals and picks out earthquakes, distinguishing them from noise, quarry blasts, and passing trucks. Seismometers record ground motion continuously, and the system must decide, in seconds, whether a wiggle is an earthquake worth reporting. The application is narrow by design: it detects seismic events, and it does nothing else. It reports what the ground did; it does not predict earthquakes, which remains beyond what the science can do.

The Problem

How can a system pick an earthquake out of continuous seismometer signals, in seconds, and tell it from noise and blasts?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect continuous waveforms from the seismometer network. Step 2: score each window for earthquake-like signals. Step 3: confirm with multiple stations, and report the event. Step 4: score five detection forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.113; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.84; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Seismic event detection picks earthquakes from continuous waveforms: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.113. Computed check: brier: 0.113; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.84; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use seismic detection to report earthquakes fast, and state the false-alarm rate alongside the detections. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Seismic detection is easy to over-claim: a model can score well on clear earthquakes and fail on the weak ones, and a false alarm that stops a train or a factory is expensive. The honest measure is detection against a catalog of known events, with the false-alarm rate stated alongside. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five detection forecasts: Brier 0.113, mean predicted probability 0.84, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: detection = waveform + model + multi-station confirmation. A seismic-detection system picks earthquake signals from continuous waveforms and confirms them across stations.

Science Proof

The science is the detection account: earthquake signals have shapes that differ from noise, and a model trained on labeled waveforms can pick them fast and accurately. The documented limit is the small event: weak signals hide in noise, and the system must balance missed events against false alarms. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: seismic detection = waveforms + detection model + confirmation. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the seismology account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.9, 1), (0.85, 1), (0.7, 0), (0.95, 1), (0.8, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Energy Load Forecasting JAH-NARAI-000036 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Energy load forecasting is the narrow-AI application that predicts how much electricity a grid will need in the next hours and days, so that generators can be scheduled and the grid stays balanced. The system reads past demand, the time of day, the weather, and the calendar, and forecasts the load ahead. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts about electricity demand, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

History

Load forecasting grew with electric grids in the twentieth century, when operators forecast demand by hand from past patterns. Statistical models came first, then machine-learning models in the 2010s, which learned demand patterns from weather and calendar data. The cited sources document energy forecasting and predictive analytics. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Energy load forecasting is the narrow-AI application that predicts how much electricity a grid will need in the next hours and days, so that generators can be scheduled and the grid stays balanced. The system reads past demand, the time of day, the weather, and the calendar, and forecasts the load ahead. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts about electricity demand, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

The Problem

How can a system forecast electricity demand hours and days ahead, well enough to schedule generators and keep the grid balanced?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect past demand, weather, and calendar data. Step 2: model demand as a function of time, temperature, and day type. Step 3: forecast the load ahead, and compare the forecast with what actually happens. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted megawatt loads against five observed loads and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 50; mean_abs_error: 24.0; mse: 760.0; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Energy load forecasting predicts electricity demand ahead: the demo’s error check gives MSE 760.0. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. Computed check: max_abs_error: 50; mean_abs_error: 24.0; mse: 760.0; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use load forecasts to schedule generation, and check the error on extreme days. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Load forecasts are easy to over-trust: a model that predicts well on ordinary days can fail badly on the days that matter, and the forecast can change the demand it predicts, because prices and behavior respond to the forecast. The honest measure is the error on the extreme days, not the average. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted megawatt loads against five observed loads give a mean squared error of 760.0, a mean absolute error of 24.0, and a largest error of 50. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real grid. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: load forecast = demand + weather + calendar. An energy-load system forecasts electricity demand ahead from past demand, weather, and the calendar.

Science Proof

The science is the demand-modeling account: electricity demand follows daily and weekly patterns, and weather, especially temperature, drives the variation. The documented limit is the unusual day: a heatwave or a holiday breaks the pattern, and the forecast fails exactly when the grid is most stressed. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: load forecasting = demand data + weather + forecast model. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the energy-forecasting account.

Livestock Health Monitoring JAH-NARAI-000037 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Livestock health monitoring is the narrow-AI application that watches farm animals through sensors and cameras, and flags the ones that may be ill: changes in movement, feeding, temperature, or behavior that signal trouble before it is visible to the eye. A farmer cannot watch every animal every day, so the system directs attention to the animals that need it. The application monitors animal health signals; it does not diagnose disease, and every alert is checked by a farmer or a veterinarian before any action is taken.

History

Precision livestock farming grew with cheap sensors in the 2010s, when farms began fitting animals with accelerometers and temperature sensors. Machine learning entered the field through models that learned normal behavior patterns and flagged deviations. The cited sources document precision livestock farming and animal health. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Livestock health monitoring is the narrow-AI application that watches farm animals through sensors and cameras, and flags the ones that may be ill: changes in movement, feeding, temperature, or behavior that signal trouble before it is visible to the eye. A farmer cannot watch every animal every day, so the system directs attention to the animals that need it. The application monitors animal health signals; it does not diagnose disease, and every alert is checked by a farmer or a veterinarian before any action is taken.

The Problem

How can a system spot illness in farm animals early, from sensor and camera signals, across a whole herd?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: fit sensors and cameras, and learn each animal’s normal behavior. Step 2: flag deviations from normal, in movement, feeding, and temperature. Step 3: send the alerts to the farmer, who checks the animal. Step 4: count the ledger. Across 8,500 animals monitored, 210 health alerts were raised, 164 illnesses were caught early, 46 alerts were false, across 32 farms. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 8500; largest_key: animals monitored; mean_value: 1790.4; smallest: 32; smallest_key: farms covered; total: 8952. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The Answer

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('animals monitored', 8500), ('health alerts', 210), ('illnesses caught early', 164), ('false alerts', 46), ('farms covered', 32)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class LivestockHealthMonitoringProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('animals monitored', 8500), ('health alerts', 210), ('illnesses caught early', 164), ('false alerts', 46), ('farms covered', 32)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Network Intrusion Detection JAH-NARAI-000038 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Network intrusion detection is the narrow-AI application that watches network traffic and flags the flows that look like an attack: unusual volumes, unusual destinations, and patterns that match known intrusions. A network carries far more traffic than any human team could read, so the system scores each flow and raises alerts on the suspicious ones. The application is narrow by design: it detects intrusions on a network, and it does nothing else. It is a defensive application: it watches for attacks so they can be stopped, and it contains no instructions for attacking anything.

History

Intrusion detection grew with computer networks in the 1980s and 1990s, when the first systems matched traffic against signatures of known attacks. Machine learning entered the field in the 2000s, when anomaly-based systems learned normal traffic patterns and flagged deviations. The cited sources document intrusion detection systems. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Network intrusion detection is the narrow-AI application that watches network traffic and flags the flows that look like an attack: unusual volumes, unusual destinations, and patterns that match known intrusions. A network carries far more traffic than any human team could read, so the system scores each flow and raises alerts on the suspicious ones. The application is narrow by design: it detects intrusions on a network, and it does nothing else. It is a defensive application: it watches for attacks so they can be stopped, and it contains no instructions for attacking anything.

The Problem

How can a system tell attack traffic from legitimate traffic, in real time, across a whole network?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: learn the network’s normal traffic patterns. Step 2: score each flow against those patterns and against known attack signatures. Step 3: raise alerts on the suspicious flows, and let analysts decide. Step 4: score five detection forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.1163; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.826; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Network intrusion detection scores traffic and alerts on the suspicious flows: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.1163. Computed check: brier: 0.1163; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.826; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use intrusion detection to watch a network defensively, and keep analysts in the loop for the final call. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Intrusion detection is an arms race: attackers adapt to the system, and a model trained on last year’s attacks may miss this year’s. The false-alarm rate is also a real cost, because every false alarm is an analyst’s time spent on legitimate traffic, and the system’s accuracy on historical data says little about its accuracy tomorrow. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five detection forecasts: Brier 0.1163, mean predicted probability 0.826, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: intrusion alert = normal traffic + deviation + signature. An intrusion-detection system scores network flows against normal patterns and known attack signatures.

Science Proof

The science is the anomaly-detection account: attacks differ from normal traffic in measurable ways, and a model trained on labeled examples can spot the difference. The documented limit is the false alarm: most alerts are legitimate traffic, and the analyst team is the bottleneck. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: intrusion detection = traffic model + scoring + analyst review. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the intrusion-detection account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.88, 1), (0.92, 1), (0.7, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.78, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Automatic Music Transcription JAH-NARAI-000039 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Automatic music transcription is the narrow-AI application that listens to a musical performance and writes it down as notes: which pitches, when they start, how long they last, and how loud they are. A musician hears a recording and wants the score, and the system produces it, note by note. The application is narrow by design: it transcribes music into notes, and it does nothing else.

History

Music transcription grew with digital audio in the 1990s, when the first systems detected pitches from recordings, and deep learning transformed the field in the 2010s, when models learned to transcribe polyphonic music, many notes at once, from labeled recordings. The cited sources document automatic music transcription. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Automatic music transcription is the narrow-AI application that listens to a musical performance and writes it down as notes: which pitches, when they start, how long they last, and how loud they are. A musician hears a recording and wants the score, and the system produces it, note by note. The application is narrow by design: it transcribes music into notes, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system listen to a musical performance and write it down as notes, when many notes sound at once?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled recordings, with the notes that were played. Step 2: train a model to map the audio to pitches, onsets, and durations. Step 3: write the score, and flag the uncertain notes. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted transcription scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Automatic music transcription writes performances down as notes: the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.2. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use music transcription to score recordings, and flag uncertain notes for human review. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Music transcription is easy to over-claim: a model can score well on clean piano recordings and fail on real performances, with room noise, expressive timing, and instruments it has never heard. The honest measure is accuracy on the recordings that actually need transcribing, not on the benchmark’s own cases. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted transcription scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 2.2, a mean absolute error of 1.4, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real system. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: transcription = audio + sequence model + notes. A music-transcription system maps audio to pitches, onsets, and durations as a sequence.

Science Proof

The science is the sequence-model account: music is a sequence of notes, and models that read it as a sequence, with pitch and timing together, do far better than note-by-note detectors. The documented limit is the dense passage: many notes at once, with overlapping harmonics, are where the model fails. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: music transcription = labeled recordings + sequence model + human check. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the transcription account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5.

The honest measure is accuracy on the recordings that actually need transcribing, not on the benchmark’s own cases. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Specification

  • dimensions: ledger values in the demo; counts and scores
  • care: Never present the ledger values as measurements of a real system; they are a model for this record.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One narrow application at one task: a single system doing one job.
  • A2: A sequence of tasks: one application family across many narrow jobs.
  • A3: Narrow applications across products: every product carrying its own narrow AI.

  • n_pairs: 5

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(88, 87), (76, 78), (93, 92), (81, 80), (69, 71)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class AutomaticMusicTranscriptionProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(88, 87), (76, 78), (93, 92), (81, 80), (69, 71)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(AutomaticMusicTranscriptionProgram().run())

Sign Language Recognition JAH-NARAI-000040 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Sign language recognition is the narrow-AI application that watches a person signing and turns the signs into words: hand shapes, movements, and facial expressions read by cameras, mapped to the signs of a sign language, and then to text or speech. Sign languages are full languages, with their own grammar, and the system must learn them as languages, not as codes for spoken words. The application is narrow by design: it recognizes signs, and it does nothing else.

History

Sign language recognition grew with computer vision in the 2000s, when the first systems recognized isolated signs from video, and deep learning transformed the field in the 2010s, when models learned to recognize continuous signing from labeled datasets. The cited sources document sign language recognition. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Sign language recognition is the narrow-AI application that watches a person signing and turns the signs into words: hand shapes, movements, and facial expressions read by cameras, mapped to the signs of a sign language, and then to text or speech. Sign languages are full languages, with their own grammar, and the system must learn them as languages, not as codes for spoken words. The application is narrow by design: it recognizes signs, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system watch a person signing and turn the signs into words, when meaning lives in hand shape, movement, and face together?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled videos of signing, with the signs that were made. Step 2: train a model to map the video to signs, reading hands, movement, and face together. Step 3: translate the signs into text, and flag the uncertain ones. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 1,200 signs recognized, 340 sentences were translated, with 58 recognition errors, from 120 signers, across 3 languages. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 1200; largest_key: signs recognized; mean_value: 344.2; smallest: 3; smallest_key: languages covered; total: 1721. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"signs recognized": 1200, "sentences translated": 340, "recognition errors": 58, "signers in dataset": 120, "languages covered": 3}.

The Answer

Sign language recognition turns signing into text: 1,200 signs recognized with 58 errors, across 3 languages. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 1200; largest_key: signs recognized; mean_value: 344.2; smallest: 3; smallest_key: languages covered; total: 1721.

When It Applies

Use sign language recognition to make signing accessible, and name the sign language and the signers it was tested on. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Sign language recognition is easy to over-claim: a model can score well on isolated signs from a few signers and fail on fluent signers it has never seen, and a system trained on one sign language knows nothing of another. The honest measure is accuracy on fluent signers, with the sign language named. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 1,721 with a mean of 344.2 across five entries. Of 1,200 signs recognized, 58 were errors, about 5 percent, and the errors cluster in continuous signing, where the boundaries between signs blur. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: sign = hands + movement + face + grammar. A sign-language system reads hand shape, movement, and face together and maps them to signs.

Science Proof

The science is the multimodal account: sign meaning lives in several channels at once, and a model that reads them together does far better than one that reads hands alone. The documented limit is the continuous signer: fluent signing blurs the boundaries between signs, and the model fails where the boundaries are thinnest. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: sign recognition = labeled video + multimodal model + translation. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the sign-recognition account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('signs recognized', 1200), ('sentences translated', 340), ('recognition errors', 58), ('signers in dataset', 120), ('languages covered', 3)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Emotion Recognition from Facial Expressions JAH-NARAI-000041 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Emotion recognition from facial expressions is the narrow-AI application that reads a face in a photograph or video and scores which expression it shows: a smile, a frown, a look of surprise. The application measures observable expressions in images, and that is all it measures: an expression is not a feeling, and this record makes no claim about any person’s inner state, which no system can observe. The application is narrow by design: it scores expressions in images, and it does nothing else.

History

Emotion recognition grew with the facial-action coding system of the 1970s, which catalogued the muscle movements of expression, and machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, when models learned to score expressions from labeled photographs. The cited sources document emotion recognition. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Emotion recognition from facial expressions is the narrow-AI application that reads a face in a photograph or video and scores which expression it shows: a smile, a frown, a look of surprise. The application measures observable expressions in images, and that is all it measures: an expression is not a feeling, and this record makes no claim about any person’s inner state, which no system can observe. The application is narrow by design: it scores expressions in images, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system score the expression a face shows, and what are the limits of what that score can mean?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled photographs, scored by human readers for expression. Step 2: train a model to score new faces. Step 3: report the score as an expression in an image, never as a claim about a feeling. Step 4: score five expression forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.215; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Emotion recognition scores expressions in images: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.215. It measures expressions, never feelings. Computed check: brier: 0.215; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use emotion recognition to score expressions in images, and never present a score as a fact about a feeling. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Emotion recognition is the most misused narrow application in this pair: a score about an expression is routinely presented as a fact about a feeling, and the science does not support that step. Expressions vary with culture and context, and the honest statement is about the image, never about the person’s inner state. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five expression forecasts: Brier 0.215, mean predicted probability 0.7, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: expression score = face image + classifier + human labels. An emotion-recognition system scores the expression a face shows in an image, and claims nothing about inner feelings.

Science Proof

The science is the expression-classification account: expressions can be scored from images, and the model’s accuracy is measured against human labels. The documented limit is the meaning of the score: an expression is not an emotion, and the same expression can mean different things in different people and cultures. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: emotion recognition = labeled faces + classifier + expression score. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the emotion-recognition account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.75, 1), (0.65, 1), (0.8, 0), (0.7, 1), (0.6, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Flood Risk Mapping JAH-NARAI-000042 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Flood risk mapping is the narrow-AI application that maps which areas are likely to flood, from terrain, rainfall, river levels, and past floods, and warns the places at risk. The system reads elevation maps and river gauges, learns how water has behaved in past floods, and produces a map of risk: which streets flood first, which homes are exposed, and where the water will go next. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its maps and warnings are forecasts about flooding, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

History

Flood mapping grew with hydrological models in the twentieth century, when engineers modeled water flow from terrain and rainfall. Machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, when models learned flood patterns from past events and satellite images. The cited sources document flood forecasting. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Flood risk mapping is the narrow-AI application that maps which areas are likely to flood, from terrain, rainfall, river levels, and past floods, and warns the places at risk. The system reads elevation maps and river gauges, learns how water has behaved in past floods, and produces a map of risk: which streets flood first, which homes are exposed, and where the water will go next. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its maps and warnings are forecasts about flooding, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

The Problem

How can a system map flood risk across a region, and warn the places at risk in time to act?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect terrain, rainfall, river levels, and past flood records. Step 2: model how water flows across the terrain. Step 3: produce a risk map, and issue warnings for the places at risk. Step 4: count the ledger. Across 340 areas mapped, 58 high-risk zones were found, 124 warnings were issued, 11 were false, and 86 rivers were monitored. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 340; largest_key: areas mapped; mean_value: 123.8; smallest: 11; smallest_key: false warnings; total: 619. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"areas mapped": 340, "high-risk zones": 58, "warnings issued": 124, "false warnings": 11, "rivers monitored": 86}.

The Answer

Flood risk mapping maps flood risk and warns the places at risk: 124 warnings issued with 11 false, across 340 areas. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 340; largest_key: areas mapped; mean_value: 123.8; smallest: 11; smallest_key: false warnings; total: 619.

When It Applies

Use flood risk maps to plan and warn, and check the warnings against severe floods. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Flood maps are easy to over-trust: a map that is right on ordinary floods can be wrong on the extreme one, and a warning that cries wolf too often will be ignored when it matters. The honest measure is the warning’s accuracy on the severe floods, not the average. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 619 with a mean of 123.8 across five entries. Of 124 warnings issued, 11 were false, about 9 percent, which is the cost of early warning: the system must warn broadly to warn in time. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: flood risk = terrain + rainfall + past floods. A flood-risk system maps which areas are likely to flood and warns the places at risk.

Science Proof

The science is the hydrological account: water flows downhill, and a model that knows the terrain and the rainfall can predict where it will go. The documented limit is the extreme event: a flood larger than any in the record breaks the model, and the map fails exactly where it matters most. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: flood mapping = terrain + river data + risk map + warnings. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the flood-forecasting account.

The demo prints: count: 5; largest: 340; largest_key: areas mapped; mean_value: 123.8; smallest: 11; smallest_key: false warnings; total: 619.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('areas mapped', 340), ('high-risk zones', 58), ('warnings issued', 124), ('false warnings', 11), ('rivers monitored', 86)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
