Full working program

import hashlib
class FloodRiskMappingProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('areas mapped', 340), ('high-risk zones', 58), ('warnings issued', 124), ('false warnings', 11), ('rivers monitored', 86)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(FloodRiskMappingProgram().run())

Automated Essay Scoring JAH-NARAI-000043 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Automated essay scoring is the narrow-AI application that reads a student’s essay and scores it: organization, argument, evidence, and language, against a rubric. A teacher cannot read every essay in a large class with equal care, so the system scores the essays and flags the ones where its score disagrees with a human reader’s. The application is narrow by design: it scores essays against a rubric, and it does nothing else. It scores writing, never a student’s worth, and its scores are checked against human readers.

History

Automated essay scoring grew with the first scoring systems of the 1960s, which counted surface features, and machine learning transformed the field in the 2010s, when models learned to score essays from labeled examples. The cited sources document automated essay scoring. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Automated essay scoring is the narrow-AI application that reads a student’s essay and scores it: organization, argument, evidence, and language, against a rubric. A teacher cannot read every essay in a large class with equal care, so the system scores the essays and flags the ones where its score disagrees with a human reader’s. The application is narrow by design: it scores essays against a rubric, and it does nothing else. It scores writing, never a student’s worth, and its scores are checked against human readers.

The Problem

How can a system score an essay fairly, against a rubric, when good writing takes many forms?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect essays scored by human readers against a rubric. Step 2: train a model to score new essays. Step 3: flag the essays where the model’s score disagrees with a human reader’s, and review them. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted essay scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Automated essay scoring scores essays against a rubric: the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.8. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use essay scoring to help teachers read at scale, and review every disagreement with a human reader. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Essay scoring is easy to over-trust: a model can agree with human readers on ordinary essays and fail on the creative ones, and a score is not a judgment of a student. The honest measure is agreement with human readers, with the disagreements reviewed, not the model’s own confidence. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted essay scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 2.8, a mean absolute error of 1.6, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real classroom. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: essay score = text + rubric + human agreement. An essay-scoring system scores student essays against a rubric and flags disagreements with human readers.

Science Proof

The science is the scoring account: essay quality can be scored from text, and the model’s accuracy is measured by agreement with human readers. The documented limit is the unusual essay: a creative or unconventional essay can be good in ways the model has never seen, and the model will mark it down. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: essay scoring = labeled essays + scoring model + human review. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the essay-scoring account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(82, 84), (76, 75), (90, 89), (68, 70), (85, 83)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class AutomatedEssayScoringProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(82, 84), (76, 75), (90, 89), (68, 70), (85, 83)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(AutomatedEssayScoringProgram().run())

Resume Screening and Candidate Matching JAH-NARAI-000044 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Resume screening and candidate matching is the narrow-AI application that reads job applications and ranks them against a role’s requirements: skills, experience, and qualifications, matched to the job description. A hiring team cannot read every application with equal care, so the system ranks them and shortlists the strongest, and humans interview from the shortlist. The application is narrow by design: it ranks resumes against a role, and it does nothing else. Its rankings shape who gets interviewed, so fairness and accuracy are both part of the record.

History

Resume screening grew with applicant tracking systems in the 2000s, when employers began storing and searching applications digitally. Machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, when models learned to rank resumes from hiring histories. The cited sources document applicant tracking systems. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Resume screening and candidate matching is the narrow-AI application that reads job applications and ranks them against a role’s requirements: skills, experience, and qualifications, matched to the job description. A hiring team cannot read every application with equal care, so the system ranks them and shortlists the strongest, and humans interview from the shortlist. The application is narrow by design: it ranks resumes against a role, and it does nothing else. Its rankings shape who gets interviewed, so fairness and accuracy are both part of the record.

The Problem

How can a system rank job applications against a role’s requirements, fairly and accurately, across thousands of applicants?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: gather the role’s requirements, skills, experience, and qualifications. Step 2: rank each resume against those requirements. Step 3: shortlist the strongest, and audit the ranking for bias across groups. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 30,000 resumes screened, 1,200 were shortlisted, 96 were hired from the shortlist, with 84 screening errors, across 45 roles. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 30000; largest_key: resumes screened; mean_value: 6285.0; smallest: 45; smallest_key: roles covered; total: 31425. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"resumes screened": 30000, "candidates shortlisted": 1200, "hires from shortlist": 96, "screening errors": 84, "roles covered": 45}.

The Answer

Resume screening ranks applications against a role: 96 hires from 1,200 shortlisted, with 84 screening errors found. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 30000; largest_key: resumes screened; mean_value: 6285.0; smallest: 45; smallest_key: roles covered; total: 31425.

When It Applies

Use resume screening to rank applications at scale, with a fairness audit alongside the accuracy check. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Resume screening is the most consequential narrow application in this pair after credit scoring: a wrong ranking denies a person an interview, and a biased ranking denies it unevenly. A model can be accurate on average and unfair in its errors, and the honest measure is accuracy plus a fairness audit, never accuracy alone. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 31,425 with a mean of 6,285.0 across five entries. Of 1,200 shortlisted candidates, 96 were hired, 8 percent, and 84 screening errors were found, which is the number that matters: each error is a candidate wrongly ranked. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: ranking = resume + role requirements + audit. A resume-screening system ranks applications against a role’s requirements, audited for bias.

Science Proof

The science is the matching account: resumes can be ranked by how well they match a role’s requirements, and the model’s accuracy is measured by how many shortlisted candidates are hired. The documented limit is fairness: a model trained on past hiring inherits past bias, and the audit is the part that catches it. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: resume screening = requirements + ranking + fairness audit. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the applicant-tracking account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. History

Resume screening grew with applicant tracking systems in the 2000s, when employers began storing and searching applications digitally. Machine learning entered the field in the 2010s, when models learned to rank resumes from hiring histories. The cited sources document applicant tracking systems. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How can a system rank job applications against a role’s requirements, fairly and accurately, across thousands of applicants?

Method

Step 1: gather the role’s requirements, skills, experience, and qualifications. Step 2: rank each resume against those requirements. Step 3: shortlist the strongest, and audit the ranking for bias across groups. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 30,000 resumes screened, 1,200 were shortlisted, 96 were hired from the shortlist, with 84 screening errors, across 45 roles. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 30000; largest_key: resumes screened; mean_value: 6285.0; smallest: 45; smallest_key: roles covered; total: 31425. Skeptical analysis

Resume screening is the most consequential narrow application in this pair after credit scoring: a wrong ranking denies a person an interview, and a biased ranking denies it unevenly. A model can be accurate on average and unfair in its errors, and the honest measure is accuracy plus a fairness audit, never accuracy alone. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('resumes screened', 30000), ('candidates shortlisted', 1200), ('hires from shortlist', 96), ('screening errors', 84), ('roles covered', 45)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class ResumeScreeningAndCandidateMatchingProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('resumes screened', 30000), ('candidates shortlisted', 1200), ('hires from shortlist', 96), ('screening errors', 84), ('roles covered', 45)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)

            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")

Airline Delay Prediction JAH-NARAI-000045 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Airline delay prediction is the narrow-AI application that forecasts whether a flight will be delayed, and by how long, from the schedule, the weather, the aircraft’s history, and the congestion at both airports. Airlines and passengers plan around the forecast: crews are assigned, gates are held, and travelers are warned. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts about flights, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

History

Flight-delay prediction grew with airline operations data in the 2000s, when carriers began mining their own delay histories. Machine-learning models entered the field in the 2010s, learning delay patterns from schedules, weather, and congestion. The cited sources document flight delays and predictive analytics. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Airline delay prediction is the narrow-AI application that forecasts whether a flight will be delayed, and by how long, from the schedule, the weather, the aircraft’s history, and the congestion at both airports. Airlines and passengers plan around the forecast: crews are assigned, gates are held, and travelers are warned. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record: its predictions are forecasts about flights, not facts, and they are judged by how far they sit from what actually happens.

The Problem

How can a system forecast whether a flight will be delayed, and by how long, from its schedule, the weather, and airport congestion?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect the flight’s schedule, weather, aircraft history, and airport congestion. Step 2: model delay as a function of those factors. Step 3: forecast the delay, and compare the forecast with what actually happens. Step 4: score five delay forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.185; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.8; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Airline delay prediction forecasts flight delays: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.185. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. Computed check: brier: 0.185; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.8; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use delay forecasts to plan crews and warn travelers, and check the error on disrupted days. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Delay forecasts are easy to over-trust: a model that predicts well on ordinary days can fail badly on the days that matter, and the forecast can change the delay it predicts, because crews and gates are reassigned in response. The honest measure is the error on the disrupted days, not the average. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five delay forecasts: Brier 0.185, mean predicted probability 0.8, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. This record is a FORECAST/PREDICTION record. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: delay forecast = schedule + weather + congestion. An airline-delay system forecasts flight delays from schedule, weather, aircraft history, and congestion.

Science Proof

The science is the delay-modeling account: delays follow patterns in weather, congestion, and aircraft rotation, and models that learn those patterns predict well under normal conditions. The documented limit is the unusual day: a storm or a system outage breaks the pattern, and the forecast fails exactly when it matters most. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: delay prediction = flight data + delay model + error check. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the flight-delay account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.8, 1), (0.7, 1), (0.85, 0), (0.9, 1), (0.75, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Smart Thermostat Climate Control JAH-NARAI-000046 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Smart thermostat climate control is the narrow-AI application that learns when a home is occupied and what temperature its people prefer, and heats or cools the home to match, saving energy when nobody is home. The thermostat reads occupancy, time, and weather, learns the household’s schedule, and adjusts the heating and cooling to fit. The application is narrow by design: it controls one home’s climate, and it does nothing else.

History

Smart thermostats grew with connected homes in the 2010s, when thermostats began learning household schedules and adjusting heating and cooling automatically. The cited sources document smart thermostats. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Smart thermostat climate control is the narrow-AI application that learns when a home is occupied and what temperature its people prefer, and heats or cools the home to match, saving energy when nobody is home. The thermostat reads occupancy, time, and weather, learns the household’s schedule, and adjusts the heating and cooling to fit. The application is narrow by design: it controls one home’s climate, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system learn a household’s schedule and preferences, and heat or cool the home to match, saving energy without sacrificing comfort?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: read occupancy, time, and weather. Step 2: learn the household’s schedule and preferred temperatures. Step 3: adjust heating and cooling to fit, and learn from manual overrides. Step 4: count the ledger. Across 12,000 homes fitted, 18 percent energy was saved, with 240 comfort complaints, 11,500 schedules learned, and 860 manual overrides. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 12000; largest_key: homes fitted; mean_value: 4923.6; smallest: 18; smallest_key: energy saved percent; total: 24618. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"homes fitted": 12000, "energy saved percent": 18, "comfort complaints": 240, "schedules learned": 11500, "manual overrides": 860}.

The Answer

Smart thermostats learn household schedules and adjust climate: 18 percent energy saved across 12,000 homes, with 860 overrides. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 12000; largest_key: homes fitted; mean_value: 4923.6; smallest: 18; smallest_key: energy saved percent; total: 24618.

When It Applies

Use smart thermostats to save energy at home, and measure comfort complaints alongside the savings. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Smart thermostats are easy to over-claim: a thermostat can save energy on average while making particular homes uncomfortable, and the savings depend on the home, the climate, and the household. The honest measure is energy saved plus comfort complaints, never energy alone. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 24,618 with a mean of 4,923.6 across five entries. Of 12,000 homes, 18 percent energy was saved, and 860 manual overrides were recorded, which is the number that matters: each override is a moment the thermostat guessed wrong. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: climate control = occupancy + schedule + weather. A smart thermostat learns a household’s schedule and adjusts heating and cooling to fit.

Science Proof

The science is the learning account: households follow patterns, and a thermostat that learns them can save energy by heating and cooling only when needed. The documented limit is the unusual day: a guest, a holiday, or a sick day breaks the pattern, and the thermostat must learn from the override rather than repeat the mistake. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: smart thermostat = sensors + learned schedule + overrides. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the smart-thermostat account.

items = [('homes fitted', 12000), ('energy saved percent', 18), ('comfort complaints', 240), ('schedules learned', 11500), ('manual overrides', 860)]

    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)

    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}

outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))

outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):

print(f"Result: {result}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SmartThermostatClimateControlProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('homes fitted', 12000), ('energy saved percent', 18), ('comfort complaints', 240), ('schedules learned', 11500), ('manual overrides', 860)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SmartThermostatClimateControlProgram().run())

Automated Document Summarization JAH-NARAI-000047 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Automated document summarization is the narrow-AI application that reads a long document and writes a short version of it: the main points, in fewer words, so a reader can grasp the document without reading it all. A report, a paper, or a contract can run to dozens of pages, and the summary is the shortcut. The application is narrow by design: it summarizes documents, and it does nothing else. It summarizes what the document says, and it must not invent what the document does not say.

History

Automatic summarization grew with the spread of digital documents in the 1990s and 2000s, when extractive systems picked the most important sentences. Abstractive systems followed, writing new sentences, and language models in the 2020s made fluent summaries routine. The cited sources document automatic summarization. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Automated document summarization is the narrow-AI application that reads a long document and writes a short version of it: the main points, in fewer words, so a reader can grasp the document without reading it all. A report, a paper, or a contract can run to dozens of pages, and the summary is the shortcut. The application is narrow by design: it summarizes documents, and it does nothing else. It summarizes what the document says, and it must not invent what the document does not say.

The Problem

How can a system read a long document and write a short version that keeps the main points and invents nothing?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: read the document and score its sentences by importance. Step 2: select the main points, or write new sentences that state them. Step 3: check the summary against the document, and flag anything the document does not support. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted summary scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Automated document summarization writes short versions of long documents: the demo’s error check gives MSE 1.6. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use document summarization to grasp long documents fast, and check the summary against the document. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Summarization is easy to over-trust: a fluent summary can look right while omitting the document’s key caveat, or inventing a point the document never made. The honest measure is the summary checked against the document, with the omissions and inventions counted, not the fluency of the prose. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted summary scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 1.6, a mean absolute error of 1.2, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real system. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: summary = main points + fewer words + no invention. A document-summarization system reads a long document and writes a short version that keeps its main points.

Science Proof

The science is the summarization account: a good summary keeps the document’s main points and drops the rest, and its quality is measured by comparison with human-written summaries. The documented limit is invention: abstractive systems can write fluent sentences the document never supported, and the check step is the part that catches it. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: summarization = importance scoring + selection + check. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the summarization account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(86, 85), (79, 80), (91, 90), (74, 76), (83, 82)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Gesture Recognition for Human-Computer Interaction JAH-NARAI-000048 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Gesture recognition for human-computer interaction is the narrow-AI application that watches a person’s hands and body and turns their movements into commands: a wave to dismiss, a point to select, a swipe to scroll. Cameras watch the gestures, and the system maps them to actions, so a person can control a device without touching it. The application is narrow by design: it recognizes gestures from a fixed set, and it does nothing else.

History

Gesture recognition grew with cameras and depth sensors in the 2000s and 2010s, when systems learned to track hands and bodies in video. The cited sources document gesture recognition. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Gesture recognition for human-computer interaction is the narrow-AI application that watches a person’s hands and body and turns their movements into commands: a wave to dismiss, a point to select, a swipe to scroll. Cameras watch the gestures, and the system maps them to actions, so a person can control a device without touching it. The application is narrow by design: it recognizes gestures from a fixed set, and it does nothing else.

The Problem

How can a system watch a person’s gestures and turn them into commands, when the same movement can mean different things?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: track the hands and body in video. Step 2: classify the movement into a gesture from a fixed set. Step 3: map the gesture to a command, and confirm the risky ones. Step 4: score five gesture forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.1299; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.836; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Gesture recognition turns hand and body movements into commands: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.1299. Computed check: brier: 0.1299; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.836; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use gesture recognition to control devices without touch, and confirm the risky commands. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Gesture recognition is easy to over-claim: a model can score well on a benchmark of clear gestures and fail on real people, with different bodies, speeds, and habits. The honest measure is accuracy on real users, with the accidental activations counted alongside the correct ones. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five gesture forecasts: Brier 0.1299, mean predicted probability 0.836, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: gesture = movement + fixed set + command. A gesture-recognition system maps hand and body movements to commands from a fixed set.

Science Proof

The science is the classification account: gestures are a fixed set of movements, and a model trained on labeled video can map movements to commands. The documented limit is ambiguity: the same movement can be a gesture or an everyday motion, and the system must tell them apart or it will act on accidents. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: gesture recognition = tracking + classification + command mapping. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the gesture-recognition account.

Demo

The demo prints: brier: 0.1299; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.836; n: 5.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.88, 1), (0.8, 1), (0.75, 0), (0.9, 1), (0.85, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
